[Russia](25391.md) has a [developing](78449.md) [market-oriented](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy) [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy) considered [high-income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank_high-income_economy) and highly industrialized.[^cite_note-GROUP-2][^cite_note-28] It has the [ninth-largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) in the world by nominal GDP and the [fourth-largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) by GDP ([PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity)).[^cite_note-IMFWEORU-7] Due to a volatile currency [exchange rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exchange_rate), its GDP measured in nominal terms fluctuates sharply.[^cite_note-29] Russia was the last major economy to join the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) (WTO), becoming a member in 2012.[^cite_note-30]

The country has large amounts of [energy resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_energy_resources) throughout its vast landmass, particularly [natural gas](22131.md) and [petroleum](63420573.md), which play a crucial role in its energy self-sufficiency and exports.[^cite_note-31] The country is a [petrostate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrostate),[^cite_note-32][^cite_note-\:1-33]<sup>: 56 </sup> with it having the [largest natural gas reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_natural_gas_proven_reserves) in the world,[^cite_note-gaz-34] the [second-largest coal reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_coal_reserves),[^cite_note-\:2-35] the [eighth-largest oil reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_proven_oil_reserves),[^cite_note-\:3-36] and the largest [oil shale reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_shale_reserves) in Europe.[^cite_note-\:4-37] Russia is the [third-largest natural gas exporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_natural_gas_exports),[^cite_note-38] the [second-largest natural gas producer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_natural_gas_production),[^cite_note-\:6-39] the [second-largest oil exporter](17808102.md)[^cite_note-\:7-40] and [producer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_oil_production),[^cite_note-\:8-41] and the third-largest coal exporter.[^cite_note-Energy_Research_2023-42] As of 2026, its [foreign exchange reserves](1015869.md) are the [fourth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_foreign-exchange_reserves) in the world.[^cite_note-auto12-43] As of 2026 few people are unemployed.[^cite_note-\:5-44] It is the [third-largest exporter of arms](608296.md) in the world.[^cite_note-45] The large oil and gas sector accounted up to 30% of Russia's federal budget revenues in 2024, down from 50% in the mid-2010s, suggesting economic diversification.[^cite_note-46]

Its [human development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_development_\(economics\)) is [ranked as](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index) "very high" in the annual [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index).[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-auto8-48] The country has the world's [twelfth-largest consumer market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_consumer_markets).[^cite_note-49] Russia has the [fifth-highest number of billionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_billionaires) in the world.[^cite_note-50] However, its [income inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_income_equality) remains comparatively high,[^cite_note-51] caused by the variance of natural resources among its [federal subjects](701438.md), leading to regional economic disparities.[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53] High [levels of corruption](27352290.md),[^cite_note-54] a shrinking labor force and labor shortages,[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] a [brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brain_drain),[^cite_note-57] and an [aging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aging_of_Russia) and [declining population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Russia) also remain major barriers to future economic growth.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59]

Following the 2022 [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), the country has faced [extensive sanctions](70323386.md) and other negative financial actions from the [Western world](21208200.md) and its allies which have the aim of isolating the Russian economy from the Western financial system.[^cite_note-60] However, Russia's economy has shown resilience to such measures broadly, and has maintained economic stability and growth driven primarily by high [military expenditure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_with_highest_military_expenditures),[^cite_note-61] rising [household consumption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Household_final_consumption_expenditure) and [wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Russian_federal_subjects_by_average_wage),[^cite_note-62] low [unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment),[^cite_note-reutersunem-14] and increased [government spending](1203070.md).[^cite_note-63] Yet, [inflation](38286.md) has remained comparatively high,[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-\:1-33]<sup>: 4 </sup> with experts predicting the sanctions will have a long-term negative effect on the Russian economy.[^cite_note-65]

## History

Main articles: [Economic history of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_the_Russian_Federation) and [Economy of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Soviet_Union)

The Russian economy has been volatile over the past multiple decades. After 1989, its institutional environment was transformed from a [command economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Command_economy) based upon [socialist organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism) to a [capitalistic system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism). Its industrial structure dramatically shifted away over the course of several years from heavy investment in [manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing) as well as in traditional Soviet [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture) towards [free market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_market) related developments in [natural gas](22131.md) and oil extraction in additional to businesses engaged in [mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining). A [service economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_economy) also expanded during this time. The academic analyst Richard Connolly has argued that, over the last four centuries in a broad sense, there were four main characteristics of the Russian economy that have shaped the system and persisted despite the political upheavals. First of all the weakness of the legal system means that impartial courts do not rule and contracts are problematic. Second is the underdevelopment of modern economic activities, with very basic peasant agriculture dominant into the 1930s. Third is technological underdevelopment, eased somewhat by borrowing from the West in the 1920s. And fourth lower living standards compared to Western Europe and North America.[^cite_note-66] In 2020 its [foreign exchange reserves](1015869.md) were the [fifth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_foreign-exchange_reserves) in the world.[^cite_note-auto12-43]

### Russian Empire

Main article: [Economy of the Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire)

This section is an excerpt from [Economy of the Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire\&action=edit)]

The economy of the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) covers the [economic history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history) of Russia from 1721 to the [October Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution) of 1917 (which ushered in a period of civil war, culminating in the creation of the Soviet Union).

Russian national income per capita increased and moved to closer to the most developed economies of Northern and Western Europe from the late 17th century to the mid 18th century.[^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:0-67][^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:1-68] After the mid 18th century, the Russian economy [stagnated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_stagnation) and declined.[^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:0-67][^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:1-68] In the 18th century, Russian national income per capita was about 40–70% of British per capita income but higher than Poland's.[^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:0-67] By 1860, Russian GDP per capita was similar to that of Japan; one-third of GDP per capita in the United States or the United Kingdom; and twice that of China or India.[^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:0-67] Russia was a late [industrializer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialisation).[^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:0-67] Over the course of the late 19th century and early 20th century, Russia's railroad system expanded considerably, enabling greater industrialization.[^cite_note-69]

[Serfdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serfdom_in_Russia), which held back development of the wage [labor market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_market) and created a shortage of labor for industry, was abolished in 1861.[^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:0-67][^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:1-68] In the aftermath, GDP per capita was volatile and did not substantially increase.[^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:0-67][^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:1-68] Steady economic growth began in the 1890s, alongside a structural transformation of the Russian economy.[^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:0-67] By the time World War I started, more than half the Russian economy was still devoted to agriculture.[^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:0-67][^cite_note-70] By the early 20th century, the Russian economy had fallen further behind the American and British economies.[^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:0-67] From the late 19th century to the early 20th century, the economy grew at a similar pace as the Japanese economy and faster than the Brazilian, Indian and Chinese economies.[^cite_note-Economy_of_the_Russian_Empire_\:0-67]

### Soviet Russia and the Soviet Union with socialist economy (1917–1987)

Main article: [Economy of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Soviet_Union)

Beginning in 1928, the course of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union)'s economy was guided by a series of [five-year plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five-year_plans_for_the_national_economy_of_the_Soviet_Union). By the 1950s, the Soviet Union had rapidly evolved from a mainly [agrarian society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarian_society) into a major industrial power.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavies1998\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmNffDWnt25wCpgPA1_1\],_\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmNffDWnt25wCpgPA3_3\]-71] By the 1970s the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) was in an [Era of Stagnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era_of_Stagnation). The complex demands of the modern economy and inflexible administration overwhelmed and constrained the central planners. The volume of decisions facing planners in [Moscow](19004.md) became overwhelming. The cumbersome procedures for bureaucratic administration foreclosed the free communication and flexible response required at the enterprise level for dealing with worker alienation, innovation, customers, and suppliers.



Workers of Moscow [Likhachev Automotive Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZiL), 1963

From 1975 to 1985, corruption and data manipulation became common practice within the [bureaucracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureaucracy) to report satisfied targets and quotas, thus entrenching the crisis.

### Economic reforms within the Soviet system, re-introduction of private property and private business (1987–1991)

Starting in 1986, [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev) attempted to address economic problems by moving towards a [market-oriented socialist economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_socialism). Gorbachev's policies of [Perestroika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika) failed to rejuvenate the Soviet economy; instead, a process of political and economic disintegration culminated in the [breakup of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) in 1991.

### Full transition to market economy, prices liberalization and privatization (1991–1997)

Main articles: [Yeltsin–Gaidar government reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeltsin%E2%80%93Gaidar_government_reforms) and [Privatization in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privatization_in_Russia)



Russian inflation rate 1993–2022



Main Directorate of the [Bank of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_Russia) for the Central Federal District



GDP per capita (PPP) in Russia, 1992–2023 (in [international dollars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_dollars))

Following the collapse of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), Russia underwent a radical transformation, moving from a [centrally planned economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy) to a [globally integrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization) [market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy). Corrupt and haphazard [privatization](24661.md) processes turned over major state-owned firms to politically connected "[oligarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_oligarch)", which has left equity ownership highly concentrated.

A [cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Boris_Yeltsin_and_Yegor_Gaidar) led by President [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin) in the place of a prime minister took office in November 1991 with a mandate to implement economic reforms. Deputy Prime Minister [Yegor Gaidar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yegor_Gaidar) led the reform program, which began on 2 January 1992 with the lifting of price controls, and caused an immediate increase in prices.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] Yeltsin's program of radical, market-oriented reform came to be known as a "[shock therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_therapy_\(economics\))". It was based on the policies associated with the [Washington Consensus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Consensus), recommendations of the [IMF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMF) and a group of top American economists, including [Larry Summers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Summers)[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-nuff-75][^cite_note-76] who in 1994 urged for "the three '-ations'—privatization, stabilization, and liberalization" to be "completed as soon as possible."[^cite_note-77]

Russian industry and society was not prepared for the change, and Yeltsin did not have control over the [Russian Central Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_Russia), which set monetary policy. As a result, monetary policy was poorly organized and coordinated. Russia experienced [hyperinflation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperinflation) between 1992 and 1995.[^cite_note-ShockTherapy-2002-78] Prices increased by 300% in January 1992,[^cite_note-Desai-1994-79] and by as much as 1,000% in the first three months.[^cite_note-ShockTherapy-2002-78] The inflation rate was 2,509% for the year 1992, 840% for 1993, 215% for 1994, and 131% for 1995.[^cite_note-ShockTherapy-2002-78]

The results were disastrous: the jump in prices from shock therapy wiped out the modest savings accumulated by Russians under socialism and resulted in a regressive redistribution of wealth in favour of elites who owned non-monetary assets.[^cite_note-80] Shock therapy was accompanied by a drop in the standard of living, including surging economic inequality and poverty,[^cite_note-81] along with increased excess mortality[^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] and a decline in life expectancy.[^cite_note-85] Russia suffered the largest peacetime rise in mortality ever experienced by an industrialized country.[^cite_note-\:9-86] Likewise, the consumption of meat decreased: in 1990, an average citizen of the RSFSR consumed 63 kg of meat a year; by 1999, it had decreased to 45 kg.[^cite_note-87] As of 1997, the number of people living in poverty had increased by fifteen times since 1990, with about half of the population below the poverty line.[^cite_note-88]

The majority of state enterprises were privatized amid great controversy and subsequently came to be owned by insiders[^cite_note-89] for far less than they were worth.[^cite_note-nuff-75] For example, the director of a factory during the Soviet regime would often become the owner of the same enterprise. Under the government's cover, outrageous financial manipulations were performed that enriched a narrow group of individuals at key positions of business and government.[^cite_note-times-90] Many of them promptly invested their newfound wealth abroad, producing an enormous capital flight.[^cite_note-91] This rapid [privatization](24661.md) of public assets, and the widespread corruption associated with it, became widely known throughout Russia as "prikhvatizatisiya," or "grab-itization."[^cite_note-92]

Difficulties in collecting government revenues amid the collapsing economy and dependence on short-term borrowing to finance budget deficits led to the [1998 Russian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Russian_financial_crisis).

In the 1990s, Russia was a major borrower from the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md), with loan facilities totalling \$20 billion. The IMF was criticised for lending so much, as Russia introduced little of the reforms promised for the money and a large part of these funds could have been "diverted from their intended purpose and included in the flows of capital that left the country illegally".[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94]

On 24 September 1993, at a meeting of the [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md) (CIS) Council of Heads of State in [Moscow](19004.md), Azerbaijan, Armenia, Belarus, Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, Moldova, Russia, Tajikistan, Uzbekistan signed the Treaty on the creation of an Economic Union which reinforces by an international agreement the intention to create an economic union through the step-by-step creation of a free trade area, a customs union and conditions for the free movement of goods, services, capital and labor. All these countries have ratified the Treaty and it entered into force on 14 January 1994. Turkmenistan[^cite_note-95] and Georgia joined in 1994 and ratified the Treaty, but Georgia withdrew in 2009.[^cite_note-96]

On 15 April 1994, at a meeting of the [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md) (CIS) Council of Heads of State in [Moscow](19004.md), all 12 post-Soviet states signed the international [Agreement on the Establishment of a Free Trade Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Independent_States_Agreement_on_the_Establishment_of_a_Free_Trade_Area) in order to move towards the creation of an economic union. Article 17 also confirmed the intention to conclude a free trade agreement in services.[^cite_note-cis.minsk.by-97] Article 1 indicated that this was "the first stage of the creation of the Economic Union", but in 1999 the countries agreed to remove this phrase from the agreement. [^cite_note-98] Russia concluded bilateral free trade agreements with all CIS countries and did not switch to a multilateral free trade regime in 1999. Bilateral free trade agreements, except for Georgia, Azerbaijan and Turkmenistan[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100] (all of these are in force as of 2024), ceased to apply only after 2012 with Russia's accession to the new multilateral CIS free trade area.

Further integration took place outside the legal framework of the CIS. Pursuant to the Treaty on the creation of an Economic Union, the Agreement on the Customs Union between the Russian Federation and the Republic of Belarus was signed on 6 January 1995 in Minsk.[^cite_note-101] The Government of the Republic of Belarus and the Government of the Russian Federation, on the one side, and the Government of the Republic of Kazakhstan, on the other side, signed an Agreement on the Customs Union in Moscow on 20 January 1995 in order to move towards the creation of an economic union as envisaged by the treaty.[^cite_note-102] The implementation of these agreements made it possible to launch the [Customs Union of the Eurasian Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Customs_Union_of_the_Eurasian_Economic_Community) in 2010. As of 2025, according to the database of international treaties of the [Eurasian Economic Union](27290106.md), these agreements are still in force and apply in part not contrary to the Treaty on the Eurasian Economic Union.[^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104]

International agreements such as the following have further deepened trade and economic relations and integration with Belarus. The Community of Belarus and Russia was founded on 2 April 1996. The "Treaty on the Union between Belarus and Russia" was signed on 2 April 1997. And finally the Treaty on the Creation of a [Union State of Russia and Belarus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_State_of_Russia_and_Belarus) was signed on 8 December 1999.[^cite_note-105]

### Early success followed by Asian and domestic financial crisis (1997–1998)

Main articles: [1997 Asian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Asian_financial_crisis) and [1998 Russian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Russian_financial_crisis)

In 1997, for the first time in many years, economic growth and low inflation were achieved. Russia had almost completed the main economic reforms necessary to complete the transition to a market economy, including the basic legislative framework, with the exception of macroeconomic stabilization. In 1997, the ruble was redenominated.

The poor situation on Asian international markets led to low oil prices, which, given the fixed and strong ruble exchange rate, did not allow for compensation for low tax collection within the country. The country's budget was not secured by revenues because the Soviet Union had virtually no taxes or fiscal system in the modern sense, which had to be created from scratch. Due to the almost fixed and strong ruble exchange rate, domestic producers suffered from cheap imports.

The combination of a large number of problems at once prevented the weak market economy from coping with the new challenges.

### Recovery of domestic production, macroeconomic stabilization and de-bureaucratization reforms (1998–2004)

Main articles: [Domestic policy of Vladimir Putin § Economic, industrial, and energy policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_Vladimir_Putin#Economic,_industrial,_and_energy_policies); and [National Priority Projects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Priority_Projects)



[Price of oil](5137675.md) in the 2000s

Between 2000 and 2002, significant pro-growth economic reforms included a comprehensive tax reform, which introduced a flat [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax) of 13%; and a broad effort at [deregulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deregulation) which benefited [small and medium-sized enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_and_medium-sized_enterprises).[^cite_note-iie-106]

Between 2000 and 2008, Russian economy got a major boost from rising commodity prices. GDP grew on average 7% per year.[^cite_note-WB_GDP_growth-107] Disposable incomes more than doubled and in [dollar](18717338.md)-denominated terms increased eightfold.[^cite_note-KPMG-108] The volume of consumer credit between 2000 and 2006 increased 45 times, fuelling a boom in private consumption.[^cite_note-vtbmagazine-109][^cite_note-samaratoday-110] The number of people living below poverty line declined from 30% in 2000 to 14% in 2008.[^cite_note-gks-111][^cite_note-CIA-112]

### Commodities boom, reserves, rise of consumer-driven economy and state corporations (2004–2008)

See also: [2000s commodities boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000s_commodities_boom)

Russia repaid its borrowing of \$3.3 billion from the IMF three years early in 2005.[^cite_note-113]

[Inflation](38286.md) remained a problem however, as the [central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Russia) aggressively expanded [money supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Money_supply) to combat appreciation of the ruble.[^cite_note-Emerging_Markets-114] Nevertheless, in 2007 the [World Bank](36752.md) declared that the Russian economy achieved "unprecedented macroeconomic stability".[^cite_note-115] Until October 2007, Russia maintained impressive fiscal discipline with budget surpluses every year from 2000.[^cite_note-iie-106]

### Modernization, regional and global integration and high-income economy (2009–2014)

Main articles: [Medvedev modernisation programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medvedev_modernisation_programme) and [Great Recession in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession_in_Russia)



Changes in the credit rating (foreign) of Russia, [Standard & Poor's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_&_Poor's)

Russian banks were affected by the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), though no long term damage was done due to a proactive and timely response by the government and central bank.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118] A sharp, but brief [recession in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession_in_Russia) was followed by a strong recovery beginning in late 2009.[^cite_note-WB_GDP_growth-107]

Between 2000 and 2012, Russia's energy exports fuelled a rapid growth in [living standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Living_standards), with real [disposable income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disposable_income) rising by 160%.[^cite_note-119] In [dollar](18717338.md)-denominated terms this amounted to a more than sevenfold increase in disposable incomes since 2000.[^cite_note-KPMG-108] In the same period, unemployment and poverty more than halved and Russians' self-assessed [life satisfaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_satisfaction) also rose significantly.[^cite_note-challenges_p12-120] This growth was a combined result of the [2000s commodities boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000s_commodities_boom), [high oil prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_oil_market_chronology_from_2003), as well as prudent economic and fiscal policies.[^cite_note-Putin_2007-121] However, these gains have been distributed unevenly, as the 110 wealthiest individuals were found in a report by Credit Suisse to own 35% of all [financial assets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_assets) held by Russian [households](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Households).[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123] Russia also has the second-largest volume of illicit money outflows, having lost over \$880 billion between 2002 and 2011 in this way.[^cite_note-124] Since 2008 [Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes) has repeatedly named [Moscow](19004.md) the "billionaire capital of the world".[^cite_note-125]

In July 2010, Russia, together with Belarus and Kazakhstan, became a founding member of the [Customs Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Customs_Union) of the [Eurasian Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Economic_Community) (EurAsEC), and the [EurAsEC Single Economic Space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Economic_Space), a [common market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_market) of the same countries, came into force on 1 January 2012, superseding the bilateral agreements on free trade. At the same time Russia's membership to the [WTO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTO) was accepted in 2011.[^cite_note-126] Russia joined the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) (WTO) on 22 August 2012 after 19 years of negotiations.[^cite_note-auto5-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] On 20 September 2012, the multi-lateral [Free Trade Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Independent_States_Free_Trade_Area) of the [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md) (CIS FTA) came into force for Russia and subsequently superseded previous bilateral agreements among 9 participating post-Soviet states.[^cite_note-auto2-130] In 2015, Russia became a founding member of the [Eurasian Economic Union](27290106.md) (EAEU), which replaced EurAsEC and envisaged a supranational [economic union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_union) (the deepest stage of [economic integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_integration)[^cite_note-auto3-131]).[^cite_note-auto-132]

Rapid GDP and income growth continued until 2013. The most important topic of discussion in the economy for a decade was the [middle-income trap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle-income_trap). In 2013, the [World Bank](36752.md) announced that Russia had graduated to a [high-income economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-income_economy) based on the results of 2012[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-WB2013-134][^cite_note-135] but in 2016 it was reclassified as an [upper-middle income economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper-middle_income_economy?action=edit\&redlink=1)[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] due to changes in the exchange rate of the Russian ruble, which is a [floating currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floating_currency). While the UN [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index), which assesses progress in the standard of living, health and education, ranks Russia among the '[very high human development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index)' countries.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139]

Russian leaders repeatedly spoke of the need to diversify the economy away from its dependence on oil and gas and foster a high-technology sector.[^cite_note-140] In 2012 oil, gas and petroleum products accounted for over 70% of total exports.[^cite_note-EIA-141] This economic model appeared to show its limits, when after years of strong performance, the Russian economy expanded by a mere 1.3% in 2013.[^cite_note-WB_GDP_growth-107] Several reasons were proposed to explain the slowdown, including a prolonged [recession in the EU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession_in_Europe), which is Russia's largest trading partner, stagnant oil prices, lack of spare industrial capacity and [demographic problems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographic_crisis_of_Russia#In_the_economic_sphere).[^cite_note-142]

### Conflicts with the West, income stagnation, import substitution programs and a pivot to the East (2014–2021)

Main articles: [Russian financial crisis (2014–2016)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_financial_crisis_\(2014%E2%80%932016\)) and [International sanctions during the Russo-Ukrainian war](43449324.md)



Countries by [natural gas proven reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_natural_gas_proven_reserves) (2014), based on data from The World Factbook. Russia has the world's largest reserves.

Following the [annexation of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Federation) in March 2014 and Russia's involvement in the ongoing [War in Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas_\(2014%E2%80%932022\)), the United States, the European Union, Canada, and Japan [imposed sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_Ukrainian_crisis) on Russia.[^cite_note-143]

According to the [Russian economic ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Economic_Development_\(Russia\)) in July 2014, GDP growth in the first half of 2014 was 1%. The ministry projected growth of 0.5% for 2014.[^cite_note-144] The Russian economy grew by a better than expected 0.6% in 2014.[^cite_note-145] Russia was rated one of the most unequal of the world's major economies.[^cite_note-146] Its [social security system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_security_system_in_Russia) comprised roughly 16% of the total GDP in 2015.[^cite_note-147]

As a result of the World Bank's designation of a high-income economy, Barack Obama issued a proclamation 9188: "I have determined that Russia is sufficiently advanced in economic development and improved in trade competitiveness that it is appropriate to terminate the designation of Russia as a beneficiary developing country effective October 3, 2014."[^cite_note-148] U.S. Customs and Border Protection (CBP) indicated that Russia formally graduated from the GSP program on 4 October 2014.[^cite_note-149]

As of 2015, [real income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_income) was still lower for 99% of Russians than it was in 1991.[^cite_note-\:9-86]



Russian bonds, [Inverted yield curves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inverted_yield_curve) to tame inflation during their wars ([Russo-Georgian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Georgian_War), [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md), [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine))&#x20;

&#x20; 20-year bond

&#x20; 10-year bond

&#x20; 1-year bond

&#x20; 3-month bond

The Russian economy risked going into recession from early 2014, mainly due to falling [oil prices](5137675.md), [sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_Ukrainian_crisis), and the subsequent [capital flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_flight).[^cite_note-150] While in 2014 GDP growth remained positive at 0.6%,[^cite_note-151] in 2015 the Russian economy shrunk by 3.7% and was expected to shrink further in 2016.[^cite_note-bloom1-152] By 2016, the Russian economy rebounded with 0.3% GDP growth and officially exited recession. The growth continued in 2017, with an increase of 1.5%.[^cite_note-gks.ru-153][^cite_note-russianeconomy2016-154]

In January 2016, [Bloomberg](432006.md) rated Russia's economy as the 12th most innovative in the world,[^cite_note-bloomberg.com-155] up from 14th in January 2015[^cite_note-156] and 18th in January 2014.[^cite_note-157] Russia has the world's 15th highest patent application rate, the 8th highest concentration of high-tech public companies, such as internet and aerospace and the third highest graduation rate of scientists and engineers.[^cite_note-bloomberg.com-155]

According to the British company BP (Statistical Yearbook 2018), proven oil reserves in Russia at the end of 2017 were 14.5 billion tonnes (14.3 billion long tons; 16.0 billion short tons), natural gas was 35 trillion cubic metres (1.2 quadrillion cubic feet).[^cite_note-158] Gold reserves in Russia's subsoil, according to the U.S. Geological Survey, were 5,500 tonnes (5,400 long tons; 6,100 short tons) at the end of 2017.[^cite_note-159]

In 2019, the [Ministry of Natural Resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Natural_Resources_and_Environment_\(Russia\)) estimated the country's mineral reserves in physical terms. At the end of 2017, oil reserves were 9.04 billion tonnes (8.90 billion long tons; 9.96 billion short tons), gas reserves were 14.47 trillion cubic metres (511 trillion cubic feet), gold reserves were 1,407 tonnes (1,385 long tons; 1,551 short tons), and diamonds reserves were 375 million carats (75 tonnes). Then for the first time the Ministry evaluated the mineral reserves of Russia in terms of value. The value of oil reserves amounted to 39.6 trillion rubles, the value of gas amounted to 11.3 trillion rubles, coking coal amounted to almost 2 trillion rubles, iron ore amounted to 808 billion rubles, diamonds amounted to 505 billion rubles, gold amounted to 480 billion rubles. The combined value of all mineral and energy resources (oil, gas, gold, copper, iron ore, thermal and lignite coal, and diamonds) amounted to 55.24 trillion rubles (US\$844 billion), or 60% of GDP for 2017. The assessment occurred after the adoption of a new classification of reserves in Russia and the object of the methodology was only those fields for which a license was issued, so the assessment of the Ministry of Natural Resources is less than the total volume of explored reserves. Experts criticized such "an unsuccessful attempt to estimate reserves," pointing out that "one should not take such an estimate seriously" and "the form contains an incorrect formula for calculating the value".[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161]

In the [International Comparison Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Comparison_Program) 2021, the Commonwealth of Independent States (CIS) region was linked through the standard global core list approach, unlike in ICP 2017. Based on the results, the [World Bank](36752.md) announced that in 2021 Russia was the 4th largest economy in the world (\$5.7 trillion and 3.8 percent of the world) and the largest economy in Europe and Central Asia when measured in PPP terms.[^cite_note-162]

### Breaking ties with the West, developing regional pan-Eurasian cooperation, tech sovereignty and self-sufficiency efforts (2022–present)

Main articles: [Economic consequences of the Russo-Ukrainian war (2022–present)](70188989.md) and [International sanctions during the Russian invasion of Ukraine](70323386.md)

In 2022, [heavy sanctions](70568070.md) were enacted due to the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) which will likely result in a steep recession.[^cite_note-163] Since early 2022, many official economic statistics have not been published.[^cite_note-164] Sanctions also included asset freezes on the [Russian Central Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Central_Bank),[^cite_note-165] which holds \$630 billion in [foreign-exchange reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_foreign-exchange_reserves),[^cite_note-166] to prevent it from offsetting the effects of sanctions.[^cite_note-167]

On 7 March 2022, the [Government of Russia](9787373.md) in the first time approved a [list of foreign states and territories that commit unfriendly acts](70389552.md) against Russia, its legal entities and individuals.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169] The Russian Federation believes that the countries that have restricted trade with Russia have directly violated the rules of the World Trade Organization. Russia distributed a statement to members of the organization, and the World Trade Organization published it on its website.[^cite_note-ru1-170] In accordance with paragraph 2 of Article 40 of the Treaty on the Eurasian Economic Union, [Russian Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Prime_Minister) [Mikhail Mishustin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Mishustin) has imposed [increased tariffs on goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Customs_Tariff) from unfriendly countries. A duty of 35% is imposed on imports of personal hygiene items, incense and weapons from unfriendly countries. The list includes shampoos and other hair products, individual deodorants and antiperspirants, products for aromatizing indoor air, detergents and cleaning products.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172] The full list of goods subject to import duties of 20-50% from unfriendly countries is established by the government decree No. 2240 of 7 December 2022, which has been amended several times in 2023–2025.[^cite_note-173]

According to most estimates, every day of the war in Ukraine costs Russia \$500 million to \$1 billion.[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176] In 2022, Russia [defaulted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_default) on part of its foreign currency, its first such default [since 1918](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repudiation_of_debt_at_the_Russian_Revolution).[^cite_note-177]

In November 2022, it was reported that Russia had officially entered a recession as the [Federal State Statistics Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_State_Statistics_Service_\(Russia\)) had reported a national GDP loss for the second consecutive quarter.[^cite_note-178]

As part of the sanctions imposed on Russia, on 2 September 2022, the finance ministers of the [G7](1196634.md) group agreed to [cap the price of Russian oil and petroleum products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Russian_crude_oil_price_cap_sanctions), designed to allow Russia to maintain production, but limiting revenue from oil sales.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180]

In 2022, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* calculated that Russia did graduate into the category of high-income economies by 2022, if counted at purchasing power parity rather than the exchange rate but could fall below the threshold due to the invasion of Ukraine.[^cite_note-181] In December 2022, a study at Bank of Russia's Research and Forecasting Department, found that the import dependence of the Russian economy is relatively low, does not exceed the median for other countries, and the share of imports in most industries is lower than in other countries. The key explanation for this could be the low involvement of the Russian economy in global value supply chains and its focus on production of raw materials. However, 60% of Russia's imports come from the countries that have announced sanctions against Russia.[^cite_note-auto1-182]

[TASS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TASS) reported poor results for the Russian economy the first quarter of 2023 with revenue of 5.7 trillion roubles – down 21% (mainly due to falling oil revenue), expenditure 8.1 trillion roubles – up 34% (mainly due to increased military costs), creating a deficit 2.4 trillion roubles – (\$29.4 billion)[^cite_note-183]

Following [Central Bank of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_Russia) interventions, the exchange rate of the rouble against the dollar remained relatively stable in 2022, although in 2023 it started to decrease significantly, reaching RUB 97 per US\$1 on 15 August 2023. Both the interventions and the exchange rate decrease resulted in significant criticism of the Central Bank by Russian state propaganda.[^cite_note-184] Quarter 2 of 2023 saw a 13% fall in the value of the rouble against the dollar and a current account surplus estimated in to be falling by 80% from the annual 2022 surplus of \$233 billion.[^cite_note-185]

After 11 years of negotiations, on 8 June 2023, in Sochi, Armenia, Belarus, Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, Russia, Tajikistan and Uzbekistan signed the [Commonwealth of Independent States Agreement on Free Trade in Services, Establishment, Operations and Investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Independent_States_Agreement_on_Free_Trade_in_Services,_Establishment,_Operations_and_Investment) to partly integrate Uzbekistan and Tajikistan on the common standards of the WTO ([General Agreement on Trade in Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Agreement_on_Trade_in_Services)) and the [EAEU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EAEU) (some provisions were borrowed from EAEU law) even without their membership in the WTO (Uzbekistan) or the EAEU (Uzbekistan and Tajikistan).[^cite_note-186]

On 19 June 2023, Lavrov stated that the [Great Eurasian Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Eurasian_Partnership) had become Russia's flagship project.[^cite_note-187]

In August and September 2023, the Central Bank of Russia started raising the key lending rate, ending up at 13% in September, while USD to RUB exchange rate remained at RUB 95.[^cite_note-188] As of June 2023 share of Russia's exports to EU dropped to 1.7% while Russia's imports from EU dropped to 1.5%.[^cite_note-189] In October 2023 the "psychological barrier" of RUB 100 per US\$1 was crossed.[^cite_note-190] In July 2024 the Russian Central Bank raised the key interest rate to 18%.[^cite_note-191]

Russia's ranking as Europe's largest economy in terms of PPP and the world's fourth largest economy was first released in May 2024.[^cite_note-192] In July 2024, the World Bank again reclassified Russia as a high-income economy based on 2023 results.[^cite_note-auto8-48]

The 2024 budget expects revenues of 35 trillion rubles (\$349 billion) with expenditure of 36.6 trillion, based on a Urals oil forecast of \$71.30 per barrel, a 90.1 rubles to US\$1 exchange rate and inflation of 4.5%. Defence spending will double to 10.78 trillion, 29.4% of expenditure. Russia currently has a record low unemployment rate of just 3 percent,[^cite_note-193] due to a demographic decline, demands of the war for industrial and military manpower, and large scale emigration.

In January 2025, it was reported that Russia had used a two-prong strategy to finance the large costs of the [Russo-Ukrainian war](42085878.md) since early 2022. In addition to the official Russian government [defense budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_budget_of_Russia)—direct financial expenditure for waging the war was estimated at US\$250 billion through June 2024,[^cite_note-ntv20240623-194] rising to over 20% of annual GDP—an off-budget financing mechanism was employed to fund the war with over US\$200 billion from preferential bank loans made to defence contractors and war-related businesses, loans compelled by the Russian government.[^cite_note-mt20250114-195][^cite_note-kpost20250115-196] Defense and security spending accounted for approximately 40% of Russia's total government spending in 2025,[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198] exceeding combined spending on education, healthcare, social policy and the national economy.[^cite_note-199]

Despite experiencing significant expansions in both 2023 and 2024, analysts have warned that the Russian economy risks stagnation in 2025 and onward due to labor shortages, high inflation, military overspending, falling resource prices and a dwindling National Wealth Fund.[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203]

The Iran-EAEU FTA came into force on 15 May 2025.[^cite_note-204] The [Mongolia](19271.md)-EAEU Interim free trade in goods agreement signed on 27 June 2025.[^cite_note-gov.kz-205][^cite_note-neg.by-206] The [United Arab Emirates](69328.md)-EAEU free trade in goods agreement signed on 27 June 2025.[^cite_note-gov.kz-205][^cite_note-neg.by-206]

On 7 August 2025, Russia signed a free trade agreement in services and investments with the UAE.[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208]

## Data

Original data are obtained and published in the national currency of the Russian Federation, the Russian ruble, by the [Federal State Statistics Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_State_Statistics_Service) Rosstat, as well as other organizations of which Russia is a member, such as the [Eurasian Economic Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Economic_Commission) and the Statistical Committee of the [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md). Due to the conversion of values into other currencies, the volume in other nominal currencies may fluctuate sharply. The average annual exchange rate of the ruble is usually used to convert annual data. The World Bank uses the [Atlas method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlas_method) (a three-year average) to estimate [GNI](1242956.md). Purchasing power parities is calculated as part of the successive cycles of the [World Bank](36752.md)'s [International Comparison Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Comparison_Program) and comes into effect after several years (ICP 2021 data were published in May 2024[^cite_note-209] and applies by the World Bank since July 2024 and by the IMF since October 2024).

## Currency and monetary policy

See also: [International reserves of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reserves_of_Russia)

On 25 December 1993, the [Constitution of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_Russian_Federation) came into force, where the main provisions were prescribed in the Article 75. The currency in the Russian Federation is the [ruble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_ruble). Monetary emission is carried out exclusively by the [Central Bank of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_the_Russian_Federation) (the Bank of Russia). The introduction and emission of other money in the Russian Federation is not allowed. The protection and ensuring the stability of the ruble is the main function of the Central Bank of the Russian Federation, which it carries out independently of other government bodies.[^cite_note-212] On 1 September 2013, the Bank of Russia also became a regulator of financial markets, applying the integrated model of financial sector supervision.[^cite_note-213]

The [Central Bank of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_the_Russian_Federation) follows [inflation targeting](2683415.md) policy. Higher inflation than in developed countries has remained throughout the last 25–30 post-Soviet years and the devaluation of the currency (in relation to foreign currencies and in relation to domestic goods) is significantly compensated by higher interest rates and an increase in nominal incomes and assets. This situation is typical for developing markets. Typically devaluations of the ruble relative to foreign currency strongly stimulate the export-oriented economy of Russia.

- Unemployment rate of Russia since the fall of the Soviet Union

  Unemployment rate of Russia since the fall of the Soviet Union

- Russian inflation rate 2012–2022

  Russian inflation rate 2012–2022

### Foreign exchange rate

The currency exchange rate of the [Russian ruble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_ruble) is [floating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floating_exchange_rate).

In 2011, the Bank of Russia, which was then headed by [Sergei Ignatiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Ignatyev_\(economist\)), took a course towards abolishing the currency corridor and designated a transition to a floating exchange rate for the ruble. Already under the new head of the Central Bank of the Russian Federation, [Elvira Nabiullina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elvira_Nabiullina), it was planned to complete the transition to a floating exchange rate regime from 1 January 2015. The Bank of Russia dismantled the currency corridor two months earlier - on 10 November 2014, so as not to waste gold and foreign exchange reserves.[^cite_note-214]

Ruble's [Real Effective Exchange Rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Effective_Exchange_Rate?action=edit\&redlink=1) remained in 2022–25 higher and stronger than in 1994 and 1998[^cite_note-215] when the sharp devaluations happened relative to the foreign currency.

The Russian ruble is a freely convertible currency, and Russia allows the free movement of capital in Russian rubles. Within the [common market of the Commonwealth of Independent States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_market_of_the_Commonwealth_of_Independent_States), the [single market of the Eurasian Economic Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_market_of_the_Eurasian_Economic_Union), and on the basis of bilateral agreements with other countries, the free movement of capital, payments and the free convertibility of the Russian ruble are ensured.

### Banking and payment system

See also: [Mir (payment system)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mir_\(payment_system\))

Russia built its banking system from scratch and created one of the most advanced banking systems in the world.[^cite_note-216]

From 2010 to 2018, the number of cashless card transactions in Russia increased by 30 times. At the same time, Russia became the world leader in the number of secure tokenized transactions which was called the "Russian miracle", according to the [Boston Consulting Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Consulting_Group) (BCG). With tokenization, the card number is not stored on a mobile device, on the smartphone manufacturer's servers, or at the merchant. The adoption of [contactless payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contactless_payments) and mobile payments ([Apple Pay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Pay), [Samsung Pay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsung_Pay)) has also been more successful in Russia than anywhere else in the world.[^cite_note-217] In 2020, Mark Barnett, President of [Mastercard](378791.md) Europe, noted that Russia is one of the most advanced and developed payment markets in Europe.[^cite_note-218] In 2020, the "Russian miracle" continued, as the pandemic gave a boost to the development of digital technologies. Russia ranked fourth in the global ranking of countries whose population actively abandoned cash in favor of cashless payments during the pandemic.[^cite_note-219]

Sberbank became the world's leader in terms of the number of transactions processed in 2024 in the ranking compiled by the Nilson analytical agency. Over the year, the number of transactions conducted by Sberbank exceeded 52 billion, surpassing the American companies JPMorgan Chase and Worldpay.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] In October 2025, Governor Elvira Nabiullina announced that [cashless payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cashless_society) accounted for 87.5% of Russia's retail turnover in the first quarter.[^cite_note-223]

As of 1 January 2025 the [Russia's national payments system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_National_Card_Payment_System) comprises 28 payment systems, 354 money transfer operators.[^cite_note-auto10-224]

The Bank of Russia's payment system (in Russian rubles) is an integral part of the national payment system. Funds transfers are carried out by the Bank of Russia through urgent funds transfers, non-urgent funds transfers and the [Faster Payment System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faster_Payment_System_\(Russia\)) ( instant 24/7 payments for work, goods, and services, including transactions made via QR code, mobile phone number and NFC).[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226] The Faster Payments System (SBP) was launched in 2019.[^cite_note-auto10-224] In 2014, the Bank of Russia established the [National Payment Card System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Payment_Card_System) Joint Stock Company (NSPK) that launched [Mir payment system cards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mir_\(payment_system\)). NSPK also processes domestic payments made in Russia with cards of international payment systems.[^cite_note-auto10-224]

The [System for Transfer of Financial Messages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/System_for_Transfer_of_Financial_Messages) for non-Ruble transfers only within Russia (and also outside of Russia) is an equivalent of the [SWIFT financial transfer system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SWIFT). Both systems only transmit text messages. Settlements are made individually between credit institutions within the framework of correspondent relations.[^cite_note-227]
